### CONCLUSION.

Every seeker of this art <note>The "art" refers to Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art" or the "Great Work."</note> must first, with mature judgment, examine the creation, operation, and virtues of the four elements <note>The traditional Aristotelian elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> along with their actions; for if he is ignorant of their origin and nature, he will not arrive at the knowledge of the principles, nor will he recognize the true matter of the stone, <note>original: *lapis*; the Philosopher’s Stone, the goal of the alchemical process.</note> much less will he attain a good end; since every end is terminated upon its own principle. <note>In alchemical philosophy, the final result must be hidden within the initial starting materials; the "end" is a perfection of the "beginning."</note> He who well knows how he begins, will well know how he shall finish.

For the origin of the elements is chaos, <note>original: *chaos*; the primordial, unformed state of matter from which the universe was fashioned.</note> from which God, the creator of all things, created and separated the elements—an act which belongs to God alone. Indeed, from these elements, Nature produces the principles of things, and this, through the will of God, belongs to Nature alone. Afterwards, from these principles, Nature produces minerals and all things, from which the artist <note>original: *artifex*; the alchemist or practitioner who works with natural substances to accelerate or perfect natural processes.</note>—by imitating Nature—can perform many wonders, since Nature produces minerals, metals, and all kinds of things from these principles, which are **Salt**, **Sulphur**, and **Mercury**; <note>Known as the *Tria Prima* or Three Primes, these represent the three fundamental substances that make up all physical matter: Salt (the body or fixity), Sulphur (the soul or oiliness), and Mercury (the spirit or fluidity).</note> and it does not produce metals simply from the elements...

<sig>I 3</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the discourse on Sulphur by outlining the hierarchical cosmology of alchemy: God creates the elements from Chaos, Nature derives the Three Primes (Salt, Sulphur, Mercury) from those elements, and the Alchemist must understand this entire chain to succeed.</summary>
<keywords>Four Elements, Chaos, Tria Prima, Salt, Sulphur, Mercury, Philosopher's Stone, Artifex, Alchemy Theory</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the concluding argument from page 197, which explained that nature does not produce metals directly from the elements, but through intermediary principles (Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury). The text here justifies the author's detailed explanation of these transitions.</meta>

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

[from the elements] but through principles that act as a medium between the elements and the metals. Therefore, if nature herself does not do this <note>That is, create metals directly from raw elements without intermediaries.</note>, much less can **Art** <note>In this context, "Art" refers to alchemy—the human attempt to imitate or perfect natural processes through laboratory work.</note> achieve it. Not only in this specific example, but in every natural process, this intermediary arrangement must be observed. For this reason, in this treatise, we have described the elements themselves, their actions and operations, as well as the origin of the principles quite extensively—providing clearer explanations than any philosopher has given until now. We have done this so that a diligent seeker may more easily consider the specific degree of distance between the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher’s Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to be a perfected substance.</note> and the metals, and between the metals and the elements.

However, we have not written this treatise because we wish to refute the ancient philosophers. Rather, we intended to confirm their writings and to fulfill those things which they omitted; for even philosophers are but men, and they cannot perform everything with absolute precision <note>original: *ad amussim*, meaning "by the ruler" or "perfectly."</note>, nor can a single person know enough about all things. Some have even been seduced away from the direct path of nature by "miracles" or anomalies, as we read happened to **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and influential philosopher/scientist who was often cited in alchemical literature.</note>, a most wise man and philosopher. He wrote that in his time, grains of gold <note>original: *grana aurea*</note> were found between the teeth of a deceased man in a tomb.

<summary>The author explains that his detailed description of elements and principles is intended to perfect the work of ancient philosophers rather than contradict them. He warns that even great thinkers like Albertus Magnus can be distracted from the "linear path of nature" by observing strange natural anomalies.</summary>

<keywords>intermediary principles, alchemical Art, Philosopher's Stone, Albertus Magnus, nature vs art, elements to metals, grains of gold</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding "miracles" or natural anomalies that misled earlier philosophers, specifically referencing Albertus Magnus (mentioned on the previous page) and his interpretation of alchemical principles found within the human body.</meta>

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

He could not investigate the certain reason for this miracle, but attributed it to a mineral power within man; and upon this opinion, he found confirmation in the saying of **Morienus** <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist, famously said to be the teacher of the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, *“and this matter, O King, is extracted from you.”* <note>original: "et hac materia, Rex, à te extrahitur." This famous alchemical aphorism suggests that the source of the "Stone" is within the human being, which the author here argues is often misinterpreted literally.</note> But this is erroneous, for Morienus intended these things to be understood **philosophically** <note>In this context, "philosophically" means metaphorically or according to the hidden principles of nature, rather than as a literal physical extraction from the human body.</note>. For the mineral virtue is located in its own kingdom, just as the animal virtue is in its own kingdom, as we have distinguished these kingdoms in the book of the *Twelve Treatises* <note>Referring to the *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (New Chemical Light), often attributed to Michael Sendivogius, which includes twelve treatises on the principles of nature.</note> and divided them into **three kingdoms**; because each of these stands and multiplies within itself without any outside entry. 

It is indeed true that in the animal kingdom there is both **Mercury** as the matter and **Sulphur** as the power; but these are animal, not mineral. If the power of animal **Sulphur** were not in man, it would not **congeal** <note>term: <term>Congelatio</term> (congealing); in alchemy, this is the process by which a fluid, "volatile" substance is made solid and "fixed."</note> the blood—which is **Mercury**—into flesh and bones. Likewise, if the power of vegetable **Sulphur** were not in the vegetable kingdom, it would not congeal water—or vegetable **Mercury**—into plants and trees. This must also be understood to apply to the mineral kingdom. Indeed, these three Mercuries do not differ in power, nor do those three Sul—

->I 4<-

<summary>This page clarifies the distinction between the three kingdoms of nature—mineral, vegetable, and animal—arguing that while each uses the principles of Mercury and Sulphur, they operate independently and should not be literally confused.</summary>

<keywords>Three Kingdoms, Mercury, Sulphur, Morienus, congealing, mineral virtue, animal virtue, Twelve Treatises</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why gold is occasionally discovered in the mouths of deceased individuals. The author concludes that this is not a biological miracle of the human body, but a result of medical treatments involving mineral mercury that was transformed after death.</meta>

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

...sulfurs, because any sulfur has a natural power to **coagulate** <note>In alchemy, coagulation refers to the process of a liquid or volatile substance becoming solid or fixed.</note> its own Mercury; and any Mercury has the power to be coagulated by its own sulfur, but not by an external one. Why, however, gold has been found or generated between the teeth of a deceased person, this is the reason: that during the life of the deceased, **Mercury** <note>Quicksilver.</note> was introduced into that sick body by some **Physician**, either through an **ointment**, or through **turbith** <note>original: "turbit." Likely refers to Turpeth (Ipomoea turpethum), a root used as a purgative in historical medicine, often administered alongside mercury.</note>, or through another method, as is the custom and practice. It is the nature of Mercury to ascend to the mouth of the patient, and to be evacuated through sores in the mouth along with **phlegm** <note>This describes "salivation therapy," a common but toxic medical treatment where mercury was used to induce heavy drooling, believed to expel disease.</note>.

If, therefore, the sick man died during such a treatment, that Mercury, having no exit, remained in the mouth between the teeth, and that **corpse** became a natural vessel for the Mercury. Thus, enclosed for a long time, it was congealed into gold by its own sulfur through the natural heat of **putrefaction**, purified from the corrosive phlegm of the human body. But if mineral Mercury had not been introduced there, gold would never be produced. And this is a most true example: that nature, in the bowels of the earth, from the alone

->solo<-

<summary>The author provides a naturalistic alchemical explanation for gold found in corpses, attributing it to mercurial medical treatments that "ripened" into gold through the heat of decay. This refutes the idea that the human body naturally produces mineral gold.</summary>

<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury, gold, coagulation, medicine, salivation therapy, turbith, putrefaction, alchemy, minerals</keywords>

# CONCERNING SULPHUR.

**Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; refers to quicksilver.</note> alone, Nature produces gold and silver, as well as other metals, according to the condition of the place or the **matrix** <note>The "womb" or specific environment within the earth where minerals and metals were thought to grow and mature.</note>. This is because Mercury contains within itself its own **sulfur** <note>In alchemical theory, all metals were believed to be composed of two principles: Sulfur (the combustible, active principle) and Mercury (the fluid, passive principle).</note> by which it is **coagulated** <note>Solidified or fixed from a liquid or volatile state.</note> into gold—unless it is hindered by some accident, or lacks the required heat, or is not in an enclosed space. Therefore, it is not the power of "animal sulfur" <note>The author is refuting the idea that the human body's biological processes could act like mineral sulfur to transform mercury into gold.</note> that freezes Mercury into gold, but rather into flesh; for if such a power existed in humans, this would happen in all bodies, which is not the case. 

Many such miracles and accidental occurrences happen which, when not properly considered by writers, lead readers into error. A good seeker of truth, however, ought to weigh everything against the possibilities of nature; if things do not agree with nature, they must be set aside. For while there is a difference between gold and water, the difference is smaller between water and **Mercury**, and smallest of all between gold and Mercury: for the "home" <note>The underlying substance or origin.</note> of gold is Mercury, and the "home" of Mercury is water. **Sulfur**, however, is the **coagulum** <note>A thickening agent or catalyst that causes solidification.</note> of Mercury; this sulfur is indeed very difficult to prepare, but it is even more difficult to investigate, because in the sulfur of the Phi- 

<meta>catchword: Phi- (likely Philosophers/Philosophorum)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that gold is formed by the natural coagulation of mercury by its own internal sulfur within a specific environment. The author emphasizes that this is a mineral process, warning readers not to mistake accidental chemical results in the human body for biological miracles.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur-mercury theory, coagulation, matrix, alchemy, mineralogy, natural law, gold formation</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page concludes the treatise "De Sulphure" (On Sulphur), following the discussion on page 201 regarding the relationship between Mercury and gold. The author shifts from the theoretical origin of Sulphur to a concluding theological dedication.</meta>

## ON SULPHUR.

In this, the entire <term>secret</term> <note>original: "arcanum"; refers to the hidden mystery or the transformative "great secret" sought by alchemists</note> of the philosophers lies hidden, which is also contained in the innermost depths of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury is not just the liquid metal (quicksilver) but the "mother" or fluid principle of all metals</note>. Concerning its preparation—without which it is useless—we shall provide instruction at another time in the section on the third of the <term>principles</term> <note>Refers to the "Tria Prima" or three primary substances of alchemy: Sulphur (soul/fire), Mercury (spirit/water), and Salt (body/earth)</note> regarding salt, since in this place we are treating the power and origin of sulphur, rather than its practical application.

It is sufficient for the diligent student to have learned the origin of the principles here, for when the beginning is unknown, the outcome is always in doubt. In this treatise, we have addressed the investigator concerning these matters not in riddles, but in the clearest language of which we were capable and which was permitted to us. If God illuminates the mind of any seeker through this work, he shall know what a successor owes to those who came before him, as this science is always acquired through such intellects. After this clear demonstration, we return this knowledge into the bosom of God the Most High, our Creator and Lord, and we commend ourselves together with our good readers to His grace and immense mercy. To Him be praise and glory through infinite ages of ages.

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page of the treatise on Sulphur transitions from theoretical alchemy to a religious dedication, promising a future work on the principle of Salt. The author emphasizes that he has written clearly rather than in the traditional alchemical riddles, attributing all scientific success to divine illumination.</summary>

<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury, Salt, Tria Prima, alchemical principles, divine illumination, history of science, philosophical secrets</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page serves as a new preface or "Address to the Reader" following the conclusion of the treatise "On Sulphur." It provides historical context regarding the authorship of the work and the corrections made by the editor for this edition.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

This author, known as the Cosmopolite—or, as we have gathered from an anagram and from hearing the name mentioned, **Michael Sendivogius** <note>original: "Michael Sengivodius"; a renowned Polish alchemist and diplomat (1566–1636)</note> the Pole—published his previous little book under the title *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novi Luminis Chymici"; a foundational text in 17th-century alchemy</note> in such a way that it was complete in itself, with nothing lacking and nothing superfluous. However, later, having apparently changed his mind, he intended it to be the first of three works in which he would treat the three <term>principles</term> <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary substances of alchemy: Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt</note>. 

The public now possesses these two treatises. Until now, the first has been regarded by common folk—chiefly due to the errors of printers—as the work of **Jean Beguin** <note>original: "Io. Beguini"; a French chemist whose popular textbook was often confused with Sendivogius's more philosophical work</note>, while the second has been neglected. Both were extremely full of errors, especially the latter, which seemed to have been snatched up for the printing press from the author's first rough drafts. 

We have corrected these works with as much reverence as boldness. We also persuaded the printer—who wishes to serve the world of letters—to publish both under the title *New Chemical Light*. Thus, for the benefit of the public, the *New Chemical Light* might rise for you with truly "new light."

->N. A. D. M.<- <note>Likely the initials of the editor or a Latin devotional abbreviation</note>

<meta>catchword: C</meta>

<summary>This preface identifies the author of the work as the Polish alchemist Michael Sendivogius and explains that the current edition corrects previous printing errors and misattributions to Jean Beguin.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Sendivogius, New Chemical Light, Jean Beguin, alchemy, printing history, three principles, Tria Prima</keywords>

## Poet of Rimini <note>original: "P. Ariminenſis"; Augurello (c. 1441–1524) was a celebrated Italian humanist, teacher, and alchemist born in the city of Rimini.</note>

# On the Art of Making Gold <term>Chrysopoeia: From the Greek words 'chrysos' (gold) and 'poiein' (to make), this refers to the alchemical process of transmuting base metals into gold.</term>

## Three Books

<image-desc>A small decorative typographic ornament consisting of three stylized floral or leaf-like elements arranged horizontally.</image-desc>

<summary>This is the title page of Giovanni Aurelio Augurello's "Chrysopoeia," a famous Renaissance poem that blends humanist poetry with alchemical theory. This work was notably dedicated to Pope Leo X, who allegedly rewarded the author with an empty purse, suggesting that a man who knew how to make gold needed no money.</summary>
